People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know she’s right, but I have a hard time stopping.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s like I know I already looked in the box or the drawer, but I can’t quite let it go. It’s like I know I already looked in the box or the drawer, but I can’t quite let it go. I feel unsettled until I go back and check again, even when my wife is waiting and I know I should just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of stopping some disaster. It’s more that I can’t move on until I check again. I’ll look through the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already did, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it. It just nags at me until I go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking again helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, at least for a little while. When I go back and check the same box or list or drawer again, I feel like I can finally move on. I know I already looked, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it, but until I check again it keeps bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to arrange things in a specific or symmetrical way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed needing things to be even or lined up a certain way. It’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things over and over, are you trying to make sure something bad or harmful doesn't happen, or is it more about just feeling unsure or uncomfortable if you don't check?\n\nPatient: It’s more about feeling unsure or uncomfortable if I don’t check. I’m not really thinking something terrible will happen. I just keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. I can’t seem to move on until I do it again, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, is it because you're trying to avoid a specific bad outcome or is it more about relieving a general feeling of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more just to ease that uncomfortable feeling. I’m not usually picturing some specific bad thing happening. I just get stuck checking the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I can’t really move on until I do it again. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it alone, but it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things repeatedly, is it to prevent something specific from going wrong or just to feel better in general?\n\nPatient: It’s really more just to feel better in general. I’m not trying to stop some particular thing from happening. I just get stuck checking the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I have trouble moving on until I do it again. My wife is usually waiting for me to just let it be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I keep getting stuck checking things over and over. Boxes, lists, the drawers in our condo… I’ll look, and then I know I already looked, but I still feel like I have to check again before I can move on. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know she’s right, but I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted thoughts or ideas that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I can tell myself it’s probably not true, but the thought comes back anyway, and then I have trouble settling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking. I’ll go back to the same boxes, or the lists, or the drawers in the condo, even when I know I already looked. It’s not always some exact pattern, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check again. My wife will be waiting, and I know I should just leave it, but it’s hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't perform these checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get anxious if I can’t check. It’s like I know I already looked in the box or the drawer, but I can’t quite let it go. I feel unsettled until I go back and check again, even when my wife is waiting and I know I should just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of stopping some disaster. It’s more that I can’t move on until I check again. I’ll look through the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already did, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it. It just nags at me until I go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking again helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, at least for a little while. When I go back and check the same box or list or drawer again, I feel like I can finally move on. I know I already looked, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it, but until I check again it keeps bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to arrange things in a specific or symmetrical way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed needing things to be even or lined up a certain way. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I keep getting stuck checking things over and over. Boxes, lists, the drawers in our condo… I’ll look, and then I know I already looked, but I still feel like I have to check again before I can move on. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know she’s right, but I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted thoughts or ideas that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I can tell myself it’s probably not true, but the thought comes back anyway, and then I have trouble settling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking. I’ll go back to the same boxes, or the lists, or the drawers in the condo, even when I know I already looked. It’s not always some exact pattern, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check again. My wife will be waiting, and I know I should just leave it, but it’s hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't perform these checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get anxious if I can’t check. It’s like I know I already looked in the box or the drawer, but I can’t quite let it go. I feel unsettled until I go back and check again, even when my wife is waiting and I know I should just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of stopping some disaster. It’s more that I can’t move on until I check again. I’ll look through the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already did, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it. It just nags at me until I go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking again helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, at least for a little while. When I go back and check the same box or list or drawer again, I feel like I can finally move on. I know I already looked, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it, but until I check again it keeps bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to arrange things in a specific or symmetrical way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed needing things to be even or lined up a certain way. It’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things over and over, are you trying to make sure something bad or harmful doesn't happen, or is it more about just feeling unsure or uncomfortable if you don't check?\n\nPatient: It’s more about feeling unsure or uncomfortable if I don’t check. I’m not really thinking something terrible will happen. I just keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. I can’t seem to move on until I do it again, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, is it because you're trying to avoid a specific bad outcome or is it more about relieving a general feeling of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more just to ease that uncomfortable feeling. I’m not usually picturing some specific bad thing happening. I just get stuck checking the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I can’t really move on until I do it again. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it alone, but it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things repeatedly, is it to prevent something specific from going wrong or just to feel better in general?\n\nPatient: It’s really more just to feel better in general. I’m not trying to stop some particular thing from happening. I just get stuck checking the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I have trouble moving on until I do it again. My wife is usually waiting for me to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check things repeatedly, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not usually a big fear, but I get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I’ll tell myself it’s probably not true, but the thought comes back and I have a hard time settling until I check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by a fear that you might have made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I threw out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I know it’s probably unlikely, but the thought comes back and I have trouble settling down until I check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake or because you just can't be sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t feel sure enough to move on. I’ll look in the same boxes or drawers, or go over a list again, even though I know I already checked it. I just get stuck checking the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I have trouble moving on until I do it again. My wife is usually waiting for me to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check things repeatedly, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not usually a big fear, but I get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I’ll tell myself it’s probably not true, but the thought comes back and I have a hard time settling until I check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by a fear that you might have made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I threw out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I know it’s probably unlikely, but the thought comes back and I have trouble settling down until I check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake or because you just can't be sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t feel sure enough to move on. I’ll look in the same boxes or drawers, or go over a list again, even though I know I already checked it. My wife is usually waiting on me, and I know I should just leave it be, but I get stuck until I look one more time.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about being certain everything is in order or about avoiding the possibility of having made a mistake?\n\nPatient: It’s more about needing to feel certain things are in order. I’ll check the same boxes, lists, or condo drawers again even though I know I already looked. My wife may be waiting for me to just leave it be, but I have trouble moving on until I check one more time.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly mostly because you're trying to be absolutely certain or because you're afraid of what might happen if you miss something?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that I’m trying to be absolutely certain. I’ll have already checked the boxes or the list or the condo drawers, but I still don’t feel settled. My wife may be waiting for me to leave it alone, and I know I already looked, but I end up checking again before I can move on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I keep getting stuck checking things over and over. Boxes, lists, the drawers in our condo… I’ll look, and then I know I already looked, but I still feel like I have to check again before I can move on. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know she’s right, but I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted thoughts or ideas that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I can tell myself it’s probably not true, but the thought comes back anyway, and then I have trouble settling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking. I’ll go back to the same boxes, or the lists, or the drawers in the condo, even when I know I already looked. It’s not always some exact pattern, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check again. My wife will be waiting, and I know I should just leave it, but it’s hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't perform these checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get anxious if I can’t check. It’s like I know I already looked in the box or the drawer, but I can’t quite let it go. I feel unsettled until I go back and check again, even when my wife is waiting and I know I should just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of stopping some disaster. It’s more that I can’t move on until I check again. I’ll look through the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already did, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it. It just nags at me until I go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking again helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, at least for a little while. When I go back and check the same box or list or drawer again, I feel like I can finally move on. I know I already looked, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it, but until I check again it keeps bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to arrange things in a specific or symmetrical way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed needing things to be even or lined up a certain way. It’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things over and over, are you trying to make sure something bad or harmful doesn't happen, or is it more about just feeling unsure or uncomfortable if you don't check?\n\nPatient: It’s more about feeling unsure or uncomfortable if I don’t check. I’m not really thinking something terrible will happen. I just keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. I can’t seem to move on until I do it again, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, is it because you're trying to avoid a specific bad outcome or is it more about relieving a general feeling of unease?\n\nPatient: It’s more just to ease that uncomfortable feeling. I’m not usually picturing some specific bad thing happening. I just get stuck checking the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I can’t really move on until I do it again. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it alone, but it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things repeatedly, is it to prevent something specific from going wrong or just to feel better in general?\n\nPatient: It’s really more just to feel better in general. I’m not trying to stop some particular thing from happening. I just get stuck checking the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I have trouble moving on until I do it again. My wife is usually waiting for me to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check things repeatedly, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not usually a big fear, but I get these nagging thoughts that maybe I threw away something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I’ll tell myself it’s probably not true, but the thought comes back and I have a hard time settling until I check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly driven by a fear that you might have made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I threw out something important from my old teaching files, or put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I know it’s probably unlikely, but the thought comes back and I have trouble settling down until I check.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly mostly because you're afraid of making a mistake or because you just can't be sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: It’s more that I just can’t feel sure enough to move on. I’ll look in the same boxes or drawers, or go over a list again, even though I know I already checked it. My wife is usually waiting on me, and I know I should just leave it be, but I get stuck until I look one more time.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly more about being certain everything is in order or about avoiding the possibility of having made a mistake?\n\nPatient: It’s more about needing to feel certain things are in order. I’ll check the same boxes, lists, or condo drawers again even though I know I already looked. My wife may be waiting for me to just leave it be, but I have trouble moving on until I check one more time.\n\nDoctor: Is your need to check things repeatedly mostly because you're trying to be absolutely certain or because you're afraid of what might happen if you miss something?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that I’m trying to be absolutely certain. I’ll have already checked the boxes or the list or the condo drawers, but I still don’t feel settled. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I keep getting stuck checking things over and over. Boxes, lists, the drawers in our condo… I’ll look, and then I know I already looked, but I still feel like I have to check again before I can move on. My wife will be waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I know she’s right, but I have a hard time stopping.\n\nDoctor: Do you have unwanted thoughts or ideas that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I get these nagging thoughts that I might have thrown away something important from my old teaching files, or maybe put a tool I still need in the wrong box. I can tell myself it’s probably not true, but the thought comes back anyway, and then I have trouble settling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks or rituals in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly it’s the checking. I’ll go back to the same boxes, or the lists, or the drawers in the condo, even when I know I already looked. It’s not always some exact pattern, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check again. My wife will be waiting, and I know I should just leave it, but it’s hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't perform these checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get anxious if I can’t check. It’s like I know I already looked in the box or the drawer, but I can’t quite let it go. I feel unsettled until I go back and check again, even when my wife is waiting and I know I should just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of stopping some disaster. It’s more that I can’t move on until I check again. I’ll look through the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already did, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it. It just nags at me until I go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking again helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, at least for a little while. When I go back and check the same box or list or drawer again, I feel like I can finally move on. I know I already looked, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it, but until I check again it keeps bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to arrange things in a specific or symmetrical way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed needing things to be even or lined up a certain way. I’m not really thinking something terrible will happen. I just keep going back to the same boxes, lists, or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked. My wife will be waiting for me to leave it alone, but it still nags at me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things repeatedly, is it to prevent something specific from going wrong or just to feel better in general?\n\nPatient: It’s really more just to feel better in general. I’m not trying to stop some particular thing from happening. I just get stuck checking the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already looked, and I have trouble moving on until I do it again. My wife is usually waiting for me to just let it be.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that if you don't check things repeatedly, you might make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. I’ll go back to the same boxes, or the lists, or the drawers in the condo, even when I know I already looked. It’s not always some exact pattern, but I feel like I can’t move on until I check again. My wife will be waiting, and I know I should just leave it, but it’s hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you can't perform these checks or rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do get anxious if I can’t check. It’s like I know I already looked in the box or the drawer, but I can’t quite let it go. I feel unsettled until I go back and check again, even when my wife is waiting and I know I should just leave it be.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, are you trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of stopping some disaster. It’s more that I can’t move on until I check again. I’ll look through the same boxes or lists or drawers at the condo, even though I know I already did, and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it. It just nags at me until I go back.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking again helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does, at least for a little while. When I go back and check the same box or list or drawer again, I feel like I can finally move on. I know I already looked, and my wife is usually waiting for me to just leave it, but until I check again it keeps bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you need to arrange things in a specific or symmetrical way?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. It’s not something that stands out to me.\n\nDoctor: When you check things over and over, are you trying to make sure something bad or harmful doesn't happen, or is it more about just feeling unsure or uncomfortable if you don't check?\n\nPatient: It’s more about feeling unsure or uncomfortable if I don’t check.
